Assuming that the PASOK lost the elections in 1985 and that another House with another majority of the New Democracy was in place, could we have a revision of the Constitution in the direction of increasing competences? Of course not.’ So that's what you're trying to do. Indeed, according to the government plan submitted in 1975 during the review at the time, the President of the Republic was also a guarantor of the harmonious functioning of the powers of the State. This institutional image of the President of the Republic has come to light and decreased during the 1986 Review, when all the powers of the President of the Republic have been abolished, except for those relating to his institutional role as Head of State, as a symbolic leader of the Armed Forces, as a representative of the country abroad for the signing of international conventions and a number of such responsibilities, of course, including those referred to in Articles 37 and 38, namely the assignment of a government to the Chief of the Party, which has an absolute majority of seats in Parliament, then an exploratory mandate for the formation of a government and further, if no government is reached, and his participation and his contribution to the Council of Political Heads of State to find a solution and, ultimately, the appointment of a temporary or universal government to hold the elections. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u141	Thank you, Mr President. The deepening of democracy and rights and the stirring up of neoliberalism, which is the main risk to both democracy and rights, in our time. Just like the proposals we have made and the deepening of individual freedoms - see Article 5(2) on the prohibition of discrimination - as we did with the addition of guarantees and the consolidation of the regulatory content of the social rights regulations to deal with the attack on neoliberalism in this area, so we are coming today with our proposals on the new architecture of the culture to address the consequences of this neoliberalism as a political phenomenon now in the credibility and effectiveness of representative democracy. Because, ladies and gentlemen, there is a lot of talk of non-liberal democracies, of a discount in some countries of the democratic representative principle. And this criticism is based. It does not, however, recognise the fact that the main cause of this dilemma of many democracies, for the appearance of extreme right-wing populism, is the domination of anti-democratic liberalism. This, Mr Javarra, general rapporteur of the majority, is the modern challenge and the main question of the representative authority. It does not have the features it had during the period of Baimar, where mainly what was questioned was the democratic character of the culture, through the projection of an alternative authoritarian, state-sponsored model. Today, the question of democracy has as its characteristic the predominance of the economy towards politics. This is manifested in many ways. On the one hand, the preservation of whole sections of political decision away from the people's delegation. The central banks, especially the European Central Bank, are an example of the isolation of important economic decisions from the democratic principle. On the other hand, the attack on social rights, due to the economic logic of neoliberalism, has the same effect at the level of political legitimacy: it makes the impression to the citizens that, whatever they vote - because of the imperialisation, as I said before, of the critical economic decisions by the political democratic decision - is the economic orthodoxy that will prevail. In fact, this is coupled with the continuing drop in their standard of living and the deterioration of social rights and conquests, because of this policy. The European Commission itself, in the White Paper which she made public about the future of Europe, a year ago, said the remorseful one, said that because of this trend we are the first generation Europeans to fear that our children will have a worse life than ours. The combination of these two trends, therefore, both the direct consequence of neoliberalism, denouncing the political process, give citizens the impression that politicians do not speak their own language. This results in the emergence of the anti-democratic characteristics in so-called non-liberal democracies. We have tried to address these phenomena of questioning politics and democracy. In what way? By strengthening the elements of representativeness of our democracy, so that the House better reflects the will of the electorate, which is guaranteed by our proposal to establish simple proportionality. At the same time, we are strengthening the prestige and credibility of the political world, which has also been frustrated by the corrosive influence of neoliberalism, unfortunately in our country and because of the phenomena of interlocutory, which traditionally characterise the political system and phenomena of common criminal nature. That is why we wanted to deal with some of these consequences explicitly, for example, the phenomenon of unelected Prime Ministers, through which you are trying to pass policies that otherwise could not pass through the electorate. What happened in Doville to George Papandreou is known. How the House succumbed to accept the appointment of an unelected Prime Minister, who would have carried out exactly those decisions that were not allowed to be put to a referendum then, is a typical example. We have other provisions that strengthen the validity of the Member, especially those relating to the non-professionalisation of the parliamentary function, setting a ceiling of terms of reference. They contain precisely this regulation, because there too it has become aware that the unreliability of policy is a universal and general phenomenon and must be restored. The first category of our proposals in the architecture of the culture is the need to strengthen the House as the most representative body of popular sovereignty. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u177	Thank you very much, Mr President. Our political system is based on the democratic principle of controls and balances. Parliamentary majority, absolute or relevant, and cooperation, the regulatory role of the President of the Republic, the principle of separation of powers, the instruments of parliamentary control, the democracy of the parties referred to in Article 29, but also democracy within the parties, are only some of the tools used by the party to prevent over-concentration of power in a centre, but without leaving an institutional void that could create insolence, instability or malfunction of the culture. In order to further strengthen this principle, the New Democracy proposes, in Article 68(2), an innovative change for the constructive establishment of committees of inquiry. This strengthens democratic accountability, gives substance and content to the opposition, minor or major, upgrades parliamentarianism, arms parliamentary control with a powerful tool, protecting transparency, restoring the credibility of the political system and better functioning of the principle of controls and balances. The committee of inquiry, ladies and gentlemen, ceases to be a means of judiciary for political controversy and moves into a valuable tool of strict parliamentary control in the hands of not only the government but also counter-population. It can be proposed by at least ten Members instead of sixty and, therefore, by the minor Opposition and voted by two-fifths of all Members, creating the conditions for truly functioning, multi-speaking and strong legislative power. Our proposal has a historical root. In Article 55 of the 1927 Constitution, where it already operates successfully in several European countries, such as Article 44 of the German Grundgesetz. It also applies to the European Parliament, Article 226 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union, where one quarter of the members are sufficient to set up a committee of inquiry. The adoption by the Revision House of this New Democracy's proposal is the one-way back to the credibility of parliamentaryism and the effectiveness of our democracy. In relation to Article 32(4), with the 1974 referendum on the state system, Constantine Karamanlis put an end to instability, establishing the city of the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic. Greece, which experienced bloody wars, civil spasms, resolute bankruptcys, ethnic disasters, poverty and refugee and changed from independence in 1830 to the republic of 1974 to seven different states, thanks to the initiative of its leader and the decision of its people to establish the most solid, the most resilient and, despite the crises, the most mature democracy, that is, the longest democracy, in the history of the modern Greek state, the third Greek democracy that we are experiencing today. The fundamental balance of our culture, which has been living and growing steadily, despite the threats, problems and difficulties in the last forty-five years, has been the beginning to which I have referred, the principle of controls and balances, a principle which, by 1986, was established in the decisive role of the Prime Minister and the regulatory role of the President of the Republic, which were in complementary and mutual control. These distinct roles were defined in the 1975 Constitution wisely and harmoniously, in order to remove the country from crises it experienced in the past, due to conflicts between the powerful duplex of executive power, the king and the Prime Minister, conflicts that led us to even great national divisions. The regulatory role of the President of the Republic was spectacularly limited after the 1986 Review, perhaps satisfying on the one hand historically repellent, but leading on the other to a strong prime ministerial system. The strengthening of the respective prime minister, his important influence on the executive, legislative and, in some cases, judicial powers, have been the reason to develop other mechanisms of control and balance, such as the mass democratic parties referred to in Article 29, the active role of Parliament, the liberation of the media, the strengthening of justice, the establishment of the independent authorities. Nevertheless, the President of the Republic remained institutional, despite the limitation of his responsibilities, the symbol of unity of the nation and the opportunity for national discussions between the parties and a composition between the different concepts now represented in the national Parliaments. This result, as many times as it has been achieved, was not because of a constitutional rule, but because the respective political players demonstrated their due responsibility and the necessary maturity. Since the nine elections of the President of the Republic that we met in post-military terms, an agreement was reached between the two major power parties in 2000, 2005 and 2010, following elections. In the four elections, the President was supported either by the strong ruling party in 1975 or by the ruling party's partnership with smaller parties or independent MPs, such as in 1980, 1985 and 1995. The first election, which led to the early dismantling of the House, was the one after the 1986 Review, in the 1990 elections, where the will of the parliamentary majority and the then Chief of Constantine Mitsotakis was expressed in the first place because he sought to overcome the distortion of electoral law and create a powerless government. In the last two elections of the President of the Republic, in 2010 and 2015, the procedure for the election of the highest authority by the opposition was essentially worked out in order to achieve micro-party goals through the early withdrawal of the mandate of a democratically elected government, which happened in both cases, and in the case of Costas of Karamanlis and in the case of Antonis Samaras, they are at the most critical stage of a national effort to protect the country from an unscrupulous bankruptcy. In the case of the Government of Karamanlis, in 2009, the work of electing the President of the Republic was done by the then head of the opposition, George Papandreou, in a raw manner, eight months before the end of its term of office, with the statement that the President himself, who will be voted against before the elections, will then be voted on. In the same way, Alexis Tsipras said in 2014, in the midst of a titanic effort for the country's financial salvation, that he will propose a consensus president only after the Samara - Venizelou government has been elected to overturn it. These two recent cases demonstrate that our system, as has evolved, enables the highest rule to work with limited responsibilities to stop the democratic mandate, which the people gave to a party and a strong prime minister, with many responsibilities, to govern for four years. The distorting use of the rules of democracy to satisfy micro-party aspirations or personal political passions leads to the need to disconnect the political life of a democratically elected government from the brutal process of electing a President of the Republic, which can lead the country to early elections. This point, namely the decoupling of the President's election, is a point where almost all the political forces met and a majority of the two hundred and twenty-four Members were formed in the House. So the question that the Review House is being asked to resolve today is how that is to be achieved. SYRIZA supports the returning votes per month, with the aim of achieving a three-fifths majority until the completion of a six-month period, i.e. the possibility of even seven votes in Parliament if they are fruitless, then the President will be elected by the people. This proposal brings together a number of disadvantages. Firstly, it creates dangerous conditions of political instability in the country for six years at times that stability and national understanding may be necessary. Secondly, it conveys party controversy to the people, where it is certain that political dialogue will not turn around the President's personality, but around the current political issues, turning the President's election into a vote of confidence in the government or against the government. Thirdly, the strong democratic legitimacy of the directly elected President reinstates, from the back door, a kind of administration in the executive, which sought to eliminate the 1974 referendum and the 1986 Review. Here too, we need openness from the opposition to say that it wants to change its culture in this way, say it and ask it for approval. Fourthly, it challengs an institutional contradiction, to have a strong President legitimized by the people, but without essential responsibilities, which will continuously and humanely give him the withdrawal syndrome vis-à-vis the Prime Minister, with everything that that means in their relationship and in the functioning of the civil society. Fifthly, the direct election increases the cost of democracy and runs counter to a typical legal coma, which has not been approved by the House, that is, the revision of Article 30(1) of the Constitution, which explicitly provides for the election of the President by Parliament, and this also shows the degree of manipulation of the majority at the time and of today's Opposition. Corresponding issues of institutional voids and political instability also arise from the proposal adopted by the Change Movement for answerable and barren elections. The new Republic, which was in the past discussing the question of the direct election of the President by the people, has always linked it to one condition, the clear redefinition of its regulatory powers as a complementary and inter-electional pole of a fundamentally strong prime minister system, which needs to be re-established at the beginning of controls and balances, as we were talking about the institutionalisation of the Council of Heads of State, for example. Today, however, after the bad experience of 2009 and 2014, does it seem more realistic that the proposal we are putting forward in response to the dilemma'solving the democratically elected government for the election of a president without substantial responsibilities or election of a president by parliament and governmental stability?', 'powerful democratic legitimacy for a weak President, elected by the people, or a stable governmental cycle of the democratically elected government and elected President by Parliament, with a broad or simple majority?' The proposal enables the political forces in the first round of their efforts, with a sense of political responsibility and a spirit of national understanding, without institutional compulsion, to elect as President a strong personality, resonating to the hundreds of eighty and two hundred MPs. If this is not done, then politically the political forces that have swept it off are exposed, but without prejudice to the mandate given by the people to the government majority, and the government's cycle and political stability, at the expense of the country, are violently interrupted by extraordinary elections. The second round provides for the election of the President by an absolute or relevant majority, so that government instability, institutional gaps or additional costs are not created for democracy. We are prepared to remove the last provision on the elder, because, as we have said in committee, it is not strong in terms of arguments, as has emerged from the debate that we have, and this shows that we have no dogmatism in our approaches when we talk about the future of the country. This proposal reminds us historically of Article 67 of the historic Constitution of 1927, but it also reminds us of the applications that we also meet in several other European countries, where the final vote is held in the House or in a broader body with absolute or simple majority. Ladies and gentlemen, Greece has sought and achieved political stability, national reconciliation and, more often than ever, political consultation. Parameters that have worked positively on this constantly improving political path have clearly been the institutions, the clear roles and rules, the clearly defined responsibilities of the state institutions, but above all the quality and maturity of the political protagonists, whenever they exist. In this very quality of the protagonists and the level of political culture is invested in the New Democracy's proposal, which leaves the parties with no sign of exaggeration in the first round of election and does not restore dysfunctional models to the executive power. The President of the Republic can and must remain the embodier and the symbol of unity of the state, consensus and understanding, but not because the Constitution is forcing it, not because the rules impose it, but because the political parties really realize it and seek it. Two words, Mr President, would allow me to say on the issues raised by SYRIZA in Articles 28, 44 and 73. The historic development of the direct democracy of ancient Athens in parliament has been inevitable for practical and substantive reasons. The large social groups created in modern democracies make it practically disreputable, economically unresponsive and politically problematic the implementation of a direct democracy in the pattern of ancient Athens, even if this application attracted digital features and justified the saying of once Vice President Al Gore of the United States that the internet is the electronic avenue of returning to the classical democracy of ancient Athens. A sign of modern application that we can study is California, the United States and Switzerland, where many issues are directly decided on by the citizens. The supporters of modern e-democracy claim that the greater responsibility goes on to citizens, the more they are trained to decide more responsibly. It is, though, isn't it? The pursuit of a return to direct democracy with popular legislative initiatives and generalised referendums - the first we are discussing it, but I stress, in the second part - can potentially wake citizens up and make them more active, but does not ensure the correctness of their decisions. Apart from the demagogues of the past, which are now not easy to locate or to be extremized by the Church of the Municipality, the difficult, modern dangers are also being perpetrated. And I mention a few concerns: Who raises the question in the referendum? Because an intelligently shaped question ensures or manipulates the answer. How and how quickly can a wrong popular decision be corrected with strong democratic legitimacy, which could threaten our state entity, our national security, or our social peace? How much does a direct decision-making process cost in an economy with problems that must be flexibly, quickly and efficiently? Who and how does it guarantee full access to the Internet as an individual right when we talk about e-democracy within the meaning of Article 5A(1) and (2) of the Constitution? Who guarantees full technical access to Wi-Fi and fibre across the country, the possibility of buying computers from the most deprived citizens, their ability to use them from digital illiterates, to which usually the older age belongs? Who also guarantees protection from "fake news", false news that runs foreign centres or hackers online, namely modern demagogues, when the well-armed advanced democracies, such as the United States, cannot already be protected? Tie the WikiLeaks case, or remember the violation of personal data on Facebook on the eve of the presidential elections in the USA. We do not, however, need to go away to see the dangerous aspects of a single media-democratic system. In my opinion, it is enough to look at the 2015 demagogic referendum. It was quickly proclaimed under Article 44(2) of the Constitution by Implementing Law 4023. He posed a vague question, which was raised in the context of fiscal policy, while this is forbidden by the Constitution and even put it in English, not even in Greek. He worked for party interest in the institution of the referendum, left little time for public consultation and political debate. It caused polarization, intense division of Greek society. He did not anticipate the consequences of the choices. And finally, it has led to the implementation of a political countermeasure contrary to what the people have approved, which forced SYRIZA to return to elections, unloading half its executives and seeking the democratic legitimacy of a rational solution that would not destroy the country. This adventure, which cost around EUR 100 billion in the national economy, has shaken people's confidence in the institution of the referendum, which saw it work micro-party and internally, and then restored the responsibility of decisions in the institutions of classical representative democracy, namely the elected government and Parliament, so that we do not experience a national disaster. We can see similar experiences in Latin America or Germany as well. However, the labelling of the negative aspects of a one-dimensional, direct-democratic system does not mean that we must completely exclude some directly democratic tools which may complement and strengthen our democracy by establishing the principle of controls and balances and by encouraging citizens to become more active, but without jeopardising the dominant role of our Parliament, which is composed of representatives and not of representatives of the people, namely people who are elected not to become involved, but to decide on the basis of their conscience. This ideal combination of the Greek Constitution focuses on both Article 44, which provides for a referendum on critical national issues and a serious social issue, apart from the budgets - since in both cases Members decide - but also on Article 29 where parties' democracy and democracy are provided for in the parties. A more direct initiative by the citizens to legislate could perhaps be discussed here and we are receptive to having this debate. In no case, however, can we escape by completely releasing referendums that do not lead anywhere, but devour populism. This combination that I described to you in Greece forms an environment of semi-immediate parliamentary democracy, which can produce effective fruit, as long as what I described as rules operate from top to bottom. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u181	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, I shall confine myself to Rule 32 concerning the election of the President of the Republic. The provisions of Article 32 of the Constitution, concerning the election of the President of the Republic, are of fundamental importance, due to the constitutional provision for the dissolution of the House, in the event of a non-election of a President who, in the past, has been said several times - led to the use of the procedure at issue, on the basis of party responsibilities and electoral strategies. The current constitutional provision seeks to achieve the broadest possible parliamentary consensus in the face of the President of the Republic, as well as the direct association made by the Constitution between the role of the President of the Republic and the form of the constitution, that is to say, it is no coincidence that these provisions have been considered as the lid of the Revision of the Constitution. The choice of the reform legislator in 1986 restored the presiding character of the constitution so that the President of the Republic symbolizes the unity of the nation and does not exert political influence. The importance of this Review - I am talking about the 1986 Review - is great, mainly because the right to early dismantling of the House has been removed, until then, by the President of the Republic, invoked by the notorious "harmony with the popular feeling". Until then, of course, this provision had not been used. Since then, the President of the Republic has been the Head of State, but without any substantial powers under the Constitution. The discretionary power of the President of the Republic in our Constitution is strictly limited and may not justify the constitutional upgrading of the presidential election. The anticipation of the early dissolution of the House is thus in complete contradiction with the objective of the consensual election of the President and his super-party nature. In fact, in specific cases, referred to by previous speakers, the spirit of the Constitution was completely altered. Instead of a good consensus between the political forces in the face of the President of the Republic, we have reached the other end: Opposition took advantage of his election as a quasi-majority right without being given to it by the Constitution, of course, in order to cause the dissolution of the House prematurely and thus to force a government's political life to be stopped, so that political change can be achieved. It was mentioned by our Parliamentary Representative that from the nine elections of the President of the Republic, which took place between 1975 and 2010, the parties reached agreement in the selection of the President of the Republic only in three cases. The need, therefore, to decouple the appointment of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament has already been made, on several occasions, both in the 2001 Review Procedure and in that of 2008, without the relevant provisions being finally revised. Finally, we believe that the time has come for this great institutional change. After all, this change is now required by the great majority of Greek society. The result of the recent elections has shown in particular the consensus of the largest part of society on this institutional reform. The New Democracy had expressly announced its intention to disconnect the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament. The sole purpose of the provision of Article 32 - the 'ratio' in legal terminology - is of course not to provide the opposition with the opportunity to cause early dissolution of the House whenever it deems it appropriate, subjecting to the relevant democratic party connections, but to highlight, to the supreme office of the President of the Republic, persons with the widest possible consensus. The President of the Republic has a supra-party role and embodies national unity. The scripture and interpretation of the provision in question, which clearly stipulates as the reason for the dissolution of the House, that the President of the Republic is unable to be elected, even for what is essentially irrelevant to his election, does not, of course, serve the national unity nor is it consistent with the so-called 'internal logic' of our parliamentary system. Parliament's democracy, ladies and gentlemen, must go beyond the methods of the use of the constitutional provisions and the valuation of the institution, which, as has been demonstrated, are taking advantage of the letter of the Constitution to violate its spirit. Through these traumatic experiences for the country, the reality is that the micro-party strategies, which have nothing to do with the institution of the President of the Republic, have completely dominated. The aim was, at all times, the artificial challenge of a political deadlock, so that the country could be brought to an early election, which, of course, has proved itself by democratic principles and is yet another form of serious misuse of power. The editorial legislator was aiming at consensus, so that we would not lead to strange manipulations. Today all Parliament's wings agree that this falsification, in essence, of the Constitution must be brought to an end. It is important that, by a large majority, Article 32 comes for review in this Review House, because the political forces of the place have come to the conclusion that the vicious cycle of overthrowing political normality, which was not the aim of the Constitution, is time to stop. So today we have the common will, which is the direction which the House has inherited us in the most prosperous way. The question, therefore, which we have to deal with, is what is the best way to achieve this institutional change. In accordance with Article 30 of the Constitution, the President of the Republic shall be the regulator of the constitution and shall be elected by the Parliament for a period of five years. In the sense that the President of the Republic regulates the functions and guarantees the harmonious coexistence of powers in the city, his role as a regulator is conceptually defined. In our tradition, namely, it guarantees the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic, which is replaced by the so-called presidential systems, where the powers and role of the President differ substantially because they are not regulatory but clearly governmental. President-in-Office of the Council. - (FR) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to begin by congratulating the President-in-Office of the Council on his excellent report. The proposal of the Officerial Representation attempts to reconcile directly with the indirect election of the President and therefore presents important problems relating to the functioning and form of our culture. It introduces a new'semi-presidential' system, since, legally speaking, there are national states either directly or indirectly elected by President. It is proposed here by the Civic Opposition something intermediate, an inexpensive one, we would say, since it is not going to be accepted, that SYRIZA should fulfil both positions. In other words, it is proposed that a presidential model of civilisation, which would create legal imbalance and further division of political life, be applied to us. In particular, the proposed review by the SYRIZA, namely direct recourse to the people after possible successive insidious votes for the election of the president, may lead to the awarding of a government, possibly the awarding of political life. It will provoke the opening of a pre-election presidential campaign in search of wider party majorities than any candidate president, who - I remind you, because it must be the basis for every revision proposal - it is not intended for our citizens to govern, but to guarantee it. The President shall, as you know, exercise the executive power with the government, but power in the Parliamentary Republic, in accordance with the provisions of Rule 1, shall be elected indirectly by Parliament. They are worth the above and for an additional reason: in SYRIZA's arguments, the President's legitimacy will not be directly derived from legislative power, but will be asserted by the executive. Why is it that the President of the Republic seeks to strengthen? Is the House not enough with its genuine representation by the Greek people? Ladies and gentlemen, because I do not want to abuse time, I come to the proposal of the New Democracy, which is clear. We say that after four fruitless efforts, which are needed for the first two two two two two votes, after a hundred and fifty ones, we must address a next vote to the same House and, between the two majority, it is considered that the President of the Republic has been elected, who has gathered the majority. The counter-project is formulated: How can it be possible for the President of the Republic to symbolise unity and, at the same time, to be elected by a simple majority? The President of the Republic, with the specific constitutional features which have been attributed to him today, responds precisely to this logic of a simple majority. It is, in other words, something that corresponds to its constitutional powers, and it also does not pose a risk to civilisation because of the increased legitimacy that the direct folk mandate could provide. So his election is a matter of political practice. According to the Qualified Opposition, that is to say, it does not deprive the President of the Republic of any supra-party nature to address the people and to ask for his vote in an election, atypical party struggle against another candidate from another party, but it weakens his possible election by parliamentarians. We, ladies and gentlemen, believe in the separation of powers, which of course complement each other, but also balance out. The way this is achieved is by the rule of law and legislative decisions of the representative body on any act of executive authority. Thus, legislative power has become, as has been said,'sine qua non insurance' which prevents arbitrary action and thus strengthens and shields the fundamental principle of the rule of law. The government has headed the Prime Minister, who depends on the confidence of the parliament. Accordingly, the President of the Republic as a direct body of the executive with specific powers must be elected by legislative power, not directly addressed to the electorate to be elected. It must not, in other words, be equal to Parliament, but it must be under its control. The election of the President of the Republic by the House is part of the broader context of representative democracy and our parliamentary system and its legal position is shaped by the combination of individual elements and, in particular, by the parliamentary nature of the culture, the way it is elected, the specific responsibilities and the extent of its responsibility. The proposal for the immediate election of the President of the Republic entails a peculiar and dangerous political conflict which cannot and must not be accepted. The proposal of the New Democracy shall be fully in line with the regulatory powers of the President and shall guarantee the uninterrupted functioning of the institutions. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u182	I give the floor to Mrs. Theodora Jackson from SYRIZA.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u183	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic, as regulated by Article 32 of the Constitution, which may lead to the dismantling of the Parliament within ten days of the third vote, provided that the three-fifths of the total number of Members of Parliament are not achieved, seeks, in the current constitutional theory, to achieve the broadest possible consensus on the face of the President of the Republic and the election of persons with supra-party recognition. It is true that, at least until 1986, until the Review by which the responsibilities of the President of the Republic were significantly reduced, it was fully justified in view of the goodwill which he had until then. However, following the review of 1986, not only does the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic be sufficiently justified by the status quo and its responsibilities as regulator of the constitution, since the President of the Republic cannot dismantle Parliament, nor exercise any political political influence, but it is also considered to be inconsistent with the scope of its powers and, in essence, acts as a mechanism which does not serve the pursuit of real and genuine consensus, but rather is extinguished towards the political conflict and the early interruption of the parliamentary term. In practice, it proved very quickly that the threat of early polling rather than acting as an incentive for the processes between parties to thrive for consensus in the face of the President of the Republic was used by a majority, but also by the opposition, as a means of exercising party policy. The argument, used by Constantine Mitsotakis, 10 years after the presidential election of 1990, is characteristic to justify his attitude at the time as leader of the majority party of the New Democracy, who sought elections in order to promote self-government. Since the Constitution provides for the possibility of the election of the President of the Republic to take the country to elections, it is certain that all parties will make use of this possibility if politically they believe they can lead the country to elections. It was also expressed by part of the theory that the opposition's refusal to participate in the presidential election, precisely in order to proclaim presidential elections, not only does it not constitute circumvention of the Constitution, but it acts as a counterweight to the current government majority and the Prime Minister's superpowers. It is ultimately a new reading of the disputed arrangement, which reverses the presidential election from an area of consensus to an opposition weapon within the party opposition. The result is what has been said so far. In the nine elections to the President of the Republic, held from 1975 until today, only in three, in 2000, 2005 and 2010, the agreement of the two parties of power took place in the President's face, either because in the case of 2000 and 2005, the opposition parties then considered that it was not in their interest to hold early elections at that time or because, in the case of 2010, the PASOK had the necessary majority, that is to say, the number of votes required for the presidential election, without being served, as we all understand, the purpose of the broader consensus, but for reasons of purely short-term parties. This resulted, according to Antonis Manitakis, in the annihilation of every constitutional self-determination of the institution of the President of the Republic, his party conflict and the weakening of his symbolic unified and supra-party speech, circumventing the Constitution and using the presidential election to meet the timely party objectives. However, I want to see what the possibilities are and what is proposed by the parties in changing the way the President of the Republic is elected, which is unanimous in all that it needs to be changed. Firstly, there is the possibility of direct, direct election of the President of the Republic by the electorate. I would like to stress that the direct election of the President of the Republic by the people does not itself constitute a alteration to the parliamentary character of the constitution. This is the case in most cases, in Ireland, Austria, Finland and Portugal, where it is accepted that the direct election of the President of the Republic does not abrogate the parliamentary character of the constitution, unless it is combined with other institutional areas and interventions at the level of the Constitution. For example, this would be the case with the constitutional declaration of the President of the Republic's ability to dismantle the House at the appropriate time, that is, when it comes to its free assessment and without other conditions. This provision would indeed directly affect the provisions of Article 1(1) of the Constitution, in conjunction with Article 35, which provides for the responsibilities of the President of the Republic, as it would alter the culture and lead to a semi-Presidential system. However, I believe that this possibility of direct election is inconsistent with either the specific responsibilities of the President of the Republic, nor with the strategic direction of transition to a political system with more consensus and less conflicts, but also with the search for people to be elected with a mild unified profile that could contribute to the dismantling of extreme political conflicts in specific political or institutional circumstances, since candidates who come to the elections will be at least two and there will be situations of conflict. Secondly, the proposal is to entrust the election of the President of the Republic to a joint special assembly set up by Members, representatives of local government or representatives of other social bodies. I believe that this view should be rejected as experimentation with unseen results and it is no coincidence that this system is clearly not implemented by any central European country, the family of which is our constitution. Only in the case of Italy has the option added, let me say, to the electorate of the President of the Republic, who is purely parliamentary, three natural persons and three citizens. The third alternative is for the President of the Republic to be elected by a qualified majority, and if this is not achieved after one or two elections, for the third vote to turn the increased majority to a relative majority of the total number of Members. This proposal is also proposed by the New Democracy. It is obvious, ladies and gentlemen, that this alternative option of electing the President of the Republic further weakens the symbolic strength and prestige of the institution, strengthening the prospect of one-party support and the one-party origin of the President of the Republic and thus exacerbates the majority and pre-central character of the political system. The parallel proposal for a well-known increase in its responsibilities, such as the convening of the Political Heads of State or Government, the appointment of the Presidents of the independent authorities and the supreme judiciary and the referral of a draft adopted for reasons of constitutionality or incompatibility with European law, despite the fact that it does not conflict with the nature of the constitution as President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic, is not considered to be inconsistent with the weakening of its symbolic role, if this is shown by the relative majority of the total number of Members. Fourth alternative, which remains, is the proposal of the SYRIZA which proposes to achieve an increase of 2/3 of all Members in the first two votes and, if this is not achieved, an increased majority of 3/5 of all Members in repetitive elections, if necessary, until six months have elapsed since the start of the election process. And if, after a six-month period has elapsed and since none of the votes are held by the required 3/5, the President of the Republic is elected by the people. It is obvious, ladies and gentlemen, that the SYRIZA proposal seeks, on the one hand, to avoid the provocation of early elections for short-term reasons of party feasibility and, on the other, to ensure the consensus of a supra-party President of the Republic. I know the opposite in this proposal, which is made by the parties, namely the risk of an incomplete, endless and dead-end repetition of votes, without the required majority being achieved. However, I would like to stress that such a choice, which would be indicative of an immature political system, would entail significant political costs for parties that would take the responsibility of an endless, dead-end process. This possibility, i.e. political costs, will be the strong incentive that the political system needs to consent to the face of the President of the Republic so that this does not lead to institutional deadlocks. In addition, ladies and gentlemen, this proposal is a positive one, even if the majority required is not reached, and until the election of the new President is still in office, the outgoing President. In any case, what is seen in the debate on this thematic section is that almost all the political forces converge on the need to revise the provision in Article 32 of the Constitution in the direction of disconnecting the presidential election from the possible dissolution of Parliament and the conflicting mediation of parliamentary elections if after the third vote on the President's election the necessary majority is not achieved. The disagreement between the various groups was made with the majority to be achieved in order to elect the President of the Republic. The proposal of the New Democracy, the President may be elected in the fourth ballot, not by the enhanced vote, but by the relative majority of the total number of Members, deprives the President of the Republic of super-party character - which is consistent with his role as a regulator of the constitution - which allows him to remove the conflicts between the executive and the legislative power, namely between the government and the parliament, which, as I said, does not change, since the powers do not change and make him a party leader, that is to say, a party leader. Furthermore, this choice, i.e. the election with the relevant majority, is clearly inappropriate for the current situation, since the President of the Republic is a symbol and symbolizes the unity of the nation. Ladies and gentlemen, in conclusion, the declared political decision on the form of the constitution, which meets the consent of all of us, is the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic. It is a system that historically worked in Transmigration in a perfectly institutional way. The President-in-Office of the Council, since it is a common place for the status of a parliamentary order, must not be altered by constitutional initiatives which may result in an appeal to the civilisation of the President-in-Office. The election of the President of the Republic by a short majority means reduced democratic legitimacy, reduced social acceptance and a reduced status for the President, while his direct election by the electorate, while at the same time strengthening his or her responsibilities, results in a profound deterioration of his or her culture and an annulled his or her historically proven functionality. Both the first, that is to say the election of the President by the short-term parliamentary majority, and his election by direct recourse to the electorate, while at the same time strengthening his responsibilities, constitute a radical change in the first and subversive political change, the second, which even changes the nature of the constitution. Direct election by the people, ladies and gentlemen, is being discussed in the light of repeated attempts to elect and only as a strong incentive to achieve the necessary increased consensus, given the need for a stable parliamentary administration, particularly in crisis situations such as those experienced and experienced by our country. Ladies and gentlemen, the President of the Republic is not a person. It is an institution that symbolises the unity of society, of the nation and of the political system. Therefore, decoupling his election from the call for early elections, which are blackmail, and his election by a genuine parliamentary majority, would constitute institutional development and democratic modernisation. Thank you, Mr President.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u184	Mr Georgios Kotsiras has the floor from the New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u185	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, when I first feel the honour, as a Member of Parliament and as a lawman, to participate in this process, the highest parliamentary procedure for the revision of the Constitution, I begin by referring to the basic thinking of the New Democracy in the context of the review process, which is the institutional shield of our culture, the parliamentary stability and the creation of a modern model of effective democracy. Therefore, the reform and modernisation of the country's Charter should be carried out in order to ensure the smooth functioning of the constitution, in the context of the definition that the House has put forward, in the context of the provisions that have come to the Revision House to vote on. I shall start with the revision of Article 68(2). Our proposal for a revision of this article comes mainly to facilitate parliamentary minority voting and strengthen its rights and control work. This amendment clearly makes it easier to operate an important mechanism of parliamentary control, such as the committees of inquiry. The Committee of Inquiry shall henceforth cease to be a privilege only for the majority. This amendment is a great innovation, which no party has so far dared to propose and which promotes pluralism and strengthens minority. Obviously, what committees of inquiry the majority or opposition will propose will be judged by the parties in the House in question on the basis of the total predictions of this provision. Support for minority rights is a fundamental democratic debt, which ensures the smooth functioning of parliamentary life, which must be strengthened and stimulated. I shall set out a specific example here from our modern parliamentary history. In 1986, the New Democracy, as a political opposition, proposed the setting up of a committee of inquiry into irregularities in the management of public money. The proposal, after discussion, was voted against by a hundred and fifty-six Members and was voted for by a hundred and twenty-five Members, who, according to the current wording of Article 68(2) of the Constitution, constituted the required two-fifths majority (2/5) of all Members to set up this committee of inquiry. The proposal was rejected at that time, according to the opinion of the then co-chairment and the government, that a parliament decision is required to set up a committee of inquiry, which is taken by a majority of the Members present, which may not be less than two fifths (fifths) of the total number of Members. On the contrary, according to the opinion that the New Democracy has backed up at the time, a committee of inquiry is enough to vote in favour of this proposal from the two-fifths (fifths) of all Members, namely the majority of twenty Members. The version of the New Democracy's military opposition at the time was consistent with the relevant theory's theory's views at that time. In fact, on the occasion of a question addressed by the late Constantine Mitsotakis to professors of Constitutional and Public Law in May 1987, I refer to their names Begler, Cassimatis, Corsica, Manesis, Mavrias, Papadimitriou, Raikos, Skouris and Tsatsos - very interesting scientific considerations have been developed, with most of the opinions convening on the fact that in order to establish a committee of inquiry into foreign policy and national defence, it is sufficient that the proposal of one fifth of the Members of Parliament is to vote a hundred and twenty-fifths, i.e. two fifths (fifths) of the total number of Members. Of course, the interpretation has remained to date as it had been established and was initially interpreted. Beyond this historical regression, in order to demonstrate the democratic nature of the proposed amendment to Article 68(2), we recall - as the general rapporteur of the majority has done - the same view as the great German sociologist Max Webber. In these theoretical attempts, which are rightly intended to prevent the admissibility of a motion for a resolution by a majority of committees of inquiry, the New Democracy's proposal, by amending the relevant provision of Article 68(2) on the basis of which, from now on, the parliamentary minority may constitute two committees of inquiry, provided that the motion of at least 10 Members is voted in favour of it by two fifths (fifths) of all Members. Here it is worth pointing out the possibility of submitting this proposal and by only ten Members, which means that even small in power parties and groups will be able to submit a proposal for a committee of inquiry, in effect turning an institution that had taken a negative view in the past as a way of parliamentary control which is equal to the parliamentary history of our country. I would like to make a brief reference to Articles 37(2), 38.1 and 84.2 proposed by SYRIZA in its review proposal, assuming, as I have pointed out in committee, that these amendments are more problematic and complicated than resolve institutional problems that may arise in the course of a parliamentary procedure. In particular, the proposal referred to in Article 37(2) concerning parliamentary status is perhaps overlooked the most important aspect to be taken into account in order to answer the question of 'parliamentary or non-parliamentary Prime Minister', because it is not, and should not be, solely the parliamentary status of Prime Minister, but rather the guaranteeing of the principle of declared. The purpose of constitutional welfare cannot be the "a Priori" exclusion of those who do not have the parliamentary status, but the confirmation of the Parliament's confidence in the face of the Prime Minister and its democratic legitimacy. In this respect, the majority of the constitutionalists - see: Tsatsos, Venizelos, Chrysanogonos, Tsakirakis, Vlachopoulou - and in any case the two cases of extra-parliamentary Prime Ministers, Xenophon Zolota and Luke Papademos, cannot be judged either that they have been unconstitutionally predictable or that they have performed their duties in a non-constitutional way. I would like to make the same criticism at the heart of the procedure in the SYRIZA proposal for a constructive vote of distrust, which should necessarily be accompanied by a proposal to appoint a new Prime Minister. We believe that this proposal also contributes more to the non-stability of parliamentary life than to the stabilisation and smooth conduct of a parliamentary process and the governmental stability that each country needs. The motion of censure against the government has the meaning of its disapproval of its general policy or its specific policies. But here, there is a transfer of the initiative to form a government that will call for Parliament's confidence in the opposition, by bypassing the majority parliamentary group to propose a Prime Minister, who will then take the exploratory mandate to form a government. I therefore believe that this proposal will rather lead to political uncertainty, rather than to resolve the issue that arises from the acceptance of the motion of censure against the government, which, in any case, expresses a displeasure and a problem with regard to government policy. In fact, I think that the adoption of this proposal will eventually result in a motion of censure not because there are real reasons against the government, but because for reasons of its own, and even for reasons of impression, an opposition party that wants to support a third person will want to create conditions of imbalance and questioning to the government's party, because it is undoubtedly a target - and it must always remain - government stability. Before closing, Mr President, I would also like to make a reference to the amendment to the article on the President of the Republic, because in any case it was analysed in detail by both the rapporteur-general and the parliamentary representative and by opposition colleagues. The so-called criticism of one-party support for the President of the Republic is contradictory, because there is provision here for a multi-party procedure before the rule of a democratic majority to give the solution, where there is a total margin in these votes for a multi-party and multi-party designation of the President of the Republic. And only when the margins are exhausted, and there is a need to avoid micro-party manipulations which have been referred to above by other colleagues, then the democratic majority principle is invaded to give a solution and to protect institutions and individuals from opportunistic calculations of the minority. In any case, they have been expressed here for the great objective pursued by this regulation, namely the disconnection and non-industrialisation of the institution of the election of the President of the Republic and its disconnection from the early recourse to the polls, opinions of approved constitutionalists who do not, in general, belong to the New Democracy area. Georgios Kasimatis has argued that if a party declares that it will not vote no candidate for the Presidency of the Republic in order to lead the country to elections on the grounds that the country needs a new government, it causes the early dissolution of the House for reasons that the Constitution does not allow or allow, and furthermore, the power to dismantle the House is sceptical. On the other hand, Dimitrios Tsatsos and Costas Chrysogonos have argued that when a party in advance declares that it will not vote for the President-in-Office appointed by other parties to provoke elections and after his objective is achieved, then a settlement of the Constitution becomes an interpreter of the Constitution, as its deepest meaning is contemptuously despised. These are words of people and scientists who agree with me, in my view, the main objective that this proposal serves and the dismantling of circumstances which have in the past led to the work of a major institution, such as the President of the Republic. The proposal of the New Democracy as a whole serves the institutional armour of the Constitution and institutions, the parliamentary life and the stability of the government, in order to smoothly complete its term and not to become a bounty often of micro-political and opportunistic impressions, which can be created and will in future create many more problems than anyone who can imagine it at the time, but the history of the future will always be much more stringent. I therefore believe that in these circumstances the New Democracy proposal is a proposal in the right direction. It shields the Constitution and the institutions and takes the country forward. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u187	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, as a special rapporteur for SYRIZA, I shall refer to the proposal for a revision of Articles 42.2, 73.1 and 28.2 of the Constitution, which refer to the unity of the institutions of direct democracy. Democracy was born in ancient times as direct. However, it could not survive because of the size of the then state entities. In later times it was reborn as representative, with Parliament being a political micrograph of the trends in the people. Today the Parliamentary Republic, which operates in an environment of globalisation, rapid technological changes and concentration of wealth in the hands of a few, has signs of fatigue. Principles contained in the Constitution in practice remain empty, lacking substance. The domination of "techno-democratic" ideology has led to some kind of post-democracy, through the continuous removal of fields from the material of democratic political confrontation. On the basis of the dominant neoliberal concept, politics is subordinated to the economy and democratic confrontation to technocratic truth. Democracy is in this way anemic, much more so that the delegation of the commons to representatives is its dominant element. In this context the state mechanism itself, private bodies, supranational organisations, independent authorities, impose policies presented as a one-way route. It is therefore necessary to return politics to the forefront, to democratise the state, to obtain substance and to produce results for people and public intervention. In this direction they can support and strengthen institutions of direct democracy and initiatives. In the context of representative democracy there can be institutionalised, without altering the representative nature of democracy, but supporting it creatively and enriching it in a way that democracy becomes "democratic" and more effective, since competences are transferred to the consultation and decision of the citizens. The current constitution states that the foundation of the civil society is popular sovereignty and that all powers stem from the people. The reference to popular sovereignty, first of all, is of symbolic importance. On the basis of the representative authority, the people's powers as a electorate are limited to electing representatives either centrally in Parliament or at regional and local level. But people's sovereignty must also be given substance. The crisis in democracy has led to dismantling even the parliamentary system. Many citizens, feeling disfellowshipped and alienated from the political background, feel that decisions are made for them without them and cannot affect political things. Even the rights of election and election tend to be largely denounced. The great and growing abstention of the citizens of the exercise of the electoral right proves this, as well as the lack of interest - especially among young people - to participate as candidates in representative-type proceedings. In announcing the initiative for the Constitutional Review, then Prime Minister Alexis Tsipras established a consultation group on the revision of the Constitution, which organised discussions, lively and electronically, with institutional bodies, initiative groups and citizens. This initial consultation also reflects the direction in which citizens should be involved, even before the institutional reform process of the Constitution begins, so that it is not just a product of the special and political, but also of the people themselves. On this basis, the Constitutional Review initiative aims to enhance democracy, strengthen people's sovereignty as the foundation of the civil society, strengthen the institutions of the parliamentary culture, against external and institutional centres, so that politics is not plunged by purely neutral technology spirit and hence, it is not a matter of allegedly neutral technocratics but of the people, and of course, of activation and of course of substantial direct intervention by citizens in the field of political decision-making. It is clear that recourse to procedures, through which the people are given a direct decision and therefore with validity, shows confidence in the people, the people's criterion and, ultimately, the popular will. Under the current Constitution, citizens' direct participation in the field of legislative power is limited to consultation prior to the legislative process, but also during the legislative process through the institution of hearing the bodies. In addition to this, SYRIZA's constitutional review proposes: The supplementation of Article 44(2) in order to establish the institution of the referendum on a popular initiative. It could be called on for critical national issues at the request of five hundred thousand citizens, who have the electoral right or for digitised bills, which regulate a serious social issue, apart from budgetary issues. This proposal in the proposed parliament received 152 votes. The referendums generally do not constitute new institutions and reforms, they also apply under other constitutional regimes. Secondly, the addition to Rule 73(1), in order to establish the constitutional foundation of the people's legislative initiative, namely to recognise the possibility - with this proposal - to a hundred thousand citizens who have the electoral right to submit proposals for legislation, which will be introduced into this House for discussion, preparation and, where appropriate, voting. This proposal was received in the nominee parliament of sixty-eight votes. The position of a number of New Democracy colleagues that the New Democracy can and must therefore vote in favour of this proposal is welcome, but from what I have understood so far, it is not yet a decision of the governing party. Third proposal, adding to Article 28.2 a new paragraph that ratification of an international treaty or agreement, which provides for the transfer of sovereign powers of the State, is by referendum. This also includes decisions relating to the country's further European course and perspective, and these decisions must have the support of the people, whose will they must be integrated. This proposal was received in the nominated House by a hundred and fifty-four votes. This means that the political power is further legitimised and the structural weaknesses of the representative system are weakened. Therefore, there is a limited representation deficit, but without any need for reforms to be carried out in the representative shapes and procedures themselves, especially in the parties and the electoral system. The democratic functioning of the parties and the introduction of simple analogy are important parameters, institutional peaks in this direction. Through the burglary of the Parliament's legislative monopoly, another political culture of collective motioning is being created and the dynamics of the people's initiative emerge. In this way, even limited, the people cease to be 'a sovereign who does not exercise sovereignty' and is not 'free only when elected by the Members of Parliament'. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member hits the warning bell.]] The New Democracy, ladies and gentlemen, faces distrust and fear of strengthening democracy in view of the people being irresponsible and immature, which does not have the knowledge to decide on important issues. This is also demonstrated by the position of colleagues from the New Democracy and by the invoking of the incorrectness of the people's decisions, in order to counter the introduction of the referendum on a popular initiative. Why is that so? Is it because it connects its neoliberal perception of the economy and the social state, with a dehydrated democracy and the ideological political refusal of popular participation and intervention? SYRIZA's proposal for the thematic unity of these provisions is important, since it refers to the promotion of civilisation in common and the enrichment of democracy by means of direct democratic elements. Furthermore, it is fully compatible with the constitutionally established principle of popular sovereignty. On this basis, the SYRIZA proposal can also be voted on by colleagues from the other parties. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] I address the Members of the New Democracy, we may have another philosophy and perception - an opinion on the economy, the social state and other areas of public life, but democracy and popular sovereignty can and must be a matter of all democratic forces. Unfortunately, the New Democracy has not been mutated - to use an expression of its general rapporteur, Mr Javara - in the direction of seeking consensus. She remains faithful to her pursuit and through constitutional review, to control everything at all levels, politically, micro-politically, even constitutionally, legally. Thank you.

It is open to dialogue, open to conflict, open to confrontation, it is open, constantly, to social competition, to political competition. And that's fair and reasonable. In this context, the President of the Republic operates as a minimum consensus so that there can be a smooth functioning of the civil society as far as possible. In this sense, it is defined as the regulator of the constitution, which guarantees him this institutional role. At the same time, he is the country's representative internationally. That is something we have not mentioned in this whole debate. Let us not forget that the President of the Republic, by the constitution, represents the country internationally. It is precisely because it has this institutional role, the regulator of the country's culture and international representation, that the President of the Republic has a legal position which cannot, in any event, be identical to the parliamentary majority, nor, of course, to the political opposition in question. It should, in other words, have a supra-party role. How does this be ensured, at least until now? This is ensured by requiring the President of the Republic to have increased political legitimacy. How does the Constitution guarantee this increased political legitimacy, which must have to have this supra-party role, as we have said? By electing the President of the Republic by a qualified majority within Parliament. To date, at least more than one party is required to agree to it. This was most often the case with an agreement between the parliamentary majority and the official opposition and in 2000 and 2005 and 2010. Therefore, in fact, from its legal position, cross-party consensus is necessary in the face of the President of the Republic. What now does SYRIZA's proposal say on this matter? The SYRIZA's proposal actually comes to reconcile two conflicting demands, to find a difficult balance between two conflicting demands: on the one hand, to maintain this supra-party role of the President of the Republic, namely, to maintain this requirement of increased legitimacy and, therefore, to be elected by an increased majority, but, at the same time, to be able to satisfy another requirement, which most of Parliament's parties consider it necessary, to disconnect its election from the dissolution of the House. There is a general demand from all parties that the election of the President of the Republic cannot continue to interrupt parliamentary periods, the drafting of a government and the mandate given by the people to that government to implement its plan. So SYRIZA is trying, with his proposal, to combine these two conflicting forces. And it puts forward a proposal, which has a complex, painful process in the search for consensus, a long-lived process within the House, which can reach up to six months, where it creates a framework of pressure for the parties to be able to come to an agreement and reach a person who has a cross-party consensus. At the same time, he who will not consent, takes his political weight away from him to drag this process, which is causing this institutional inconvenience. If this consensus is not reached, it says the SYRIZA's proposal, then we go to the last resort as ultimum refugium, to appeal to the people, to the people, because in any deadlock 'at the end of the day' in the democratic state, the solution is to take the people. But this appeal to the people is that of the ultimum refugium, the ultimate refuge, the extreme case in the SYRIZA proposal. The counter-project has been heard that, with the SYRIZA proposal, even as the last option for the election of the President of the Republic by the people of the Republic, we are not allowed to increase its role and that we are making it a pole in the political system, because we are giving him the possibility of direct popular legitimacy. Here, be clear. The legal position of the President of the Republic does not change because the process is changing. Its monetary policy remains the same. Its responsibilities are the same. They are neither extended nor amended because this process is changed because it is disconnected from the dissolution of the parliament. In our proposal, therefore, we are in agreement with two opposing things: increased legitimacy - an eighty-eight Members of Parliament or, as a substitute for this increased majority, election by the people - and the non-dissolution of the House. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u197	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the Constitutional Review is the leading process of the Parliamentary Republic, even with this context, in which Parliament is called upon to revise a limited body of provisions without peaks.
