Ladies and gentlemen, on the revised provisions, Section 3, Article 37(2): Appointment of Prime Minister and Government. This was a proposal from the SYRIZA government at the time to introduce the parliamentary status for the Prime Minister as well. It is a proposal that makes democracy difficult in the event of political crises. In fact, given that the highest institution, Parliament's House, provides confidence in a government, the further conditions are unnecessary, not to say that they are indirectly deviating or, even more so, afflicting parliamentary citizens. Article 38: Government - discharge of duties. Paragraph 1. It is proposed by the committee, as in Article 84(2), to establish a constructive vote of censure. We reject it. There's no conversation left. Paragraph 2: We propose the replacement of the subparagraphs (a, (b) and (c) of Article 38 as follows: The Prime Minister shall appoint the Deputy Prime Minister at the beginning of the term of office, who shall replace him if he fails or is unable, for health reasons, to carry out his duties. The Deputy Prime Minister shall take the oath of the Republic when he is called upon to take office. I would remind you that it is a proposal by Professor Dimitris Cremastinos, because of his experience with the events of the period 1995-1996 with the most serious illness of the then Prime Minister Andreas Papandreou. This proposal was not supported by the current government in the Proposing Parliament. We invite you, gentlemen of the government, to reconsider. Article 68(2), Parliamentary and Examination Committees. The proposal for revision was tabled by the New Democracy and was voted by two hundred forty-seven Members. It is very important, I would say, for broad consensus. The most important thing, however, is that it is also the view of more than one parliamentary group. Ladies and gentlemen, we have proposed to improve the provision even further by providing that the setting-up of a committee of inquiry is also a right to a minority in the exercise of parliamentary control. We are proposing here to add as follows to the second paragraph: 'As during a parliamentary term, two committees of inquiry may be set up with a proposal from the opposition Members and in accordance with the procedure laid down in the previous subparagraph'. Fourth section of revised provisions. With regard to Articles 28(2) and 44(2) concerning the referendum, our position is that no change and no additions are needed. For everything that SYRIZA proposes, it is sufficient to consider all the questions raised by the Greek people and when the result of "No" became "Yes". The conclusions of yours. In general, the institution of the referendum needs attention and, I would say, wisdom. We see where the referendum on Brexit led, but also the referendum on the break-up of Catalan from the Spanish state. Article 73: Right to propose laws. We are positive about the possibility of exercising the popular legislative initiative on the basis of the concentration of a certain number of signatures in order to enter the House for discussion. We therefore propose that, with the signature of five hundred thousand citizens, proposals for legislation can be drawn up and tabled in the House, which, by decision of its President, are subject to discussion and which come to the House. We also propose that the proposal for a law tabled by the opposition should be introduced into the plenary agenda. We expect the proposal that we have tabled on this provision to be accepted. Eighth section of revised provisions. Article 32(4) and (5): Election of the President of the Republic. Ladies and gentlemen, the immediate election of the President of the Republic would be a disastrous mistake, a democratic regression. Let us learn from history and not ignore it. In whichever state the executive was two-headed, i.e. there was a division and the system was, in fact, a mere analogous system, instability was predetermined. It was therefore adopted to limit the responsibilities of the Head of State and its indirect election. Here in Greece we have experienced the national division. That is why I said that it is a disastrous mistake to reshape a framework which, in crisis circumstances, will favour it. Why? Because a single-faced institution, which is directly elected, even if it does not have any special competence, has itself enormous, but huge, I would say, political power. There is therefore a risk, even with a small degree of opinion between the President of the Republic and Prime Minister, that issues may arise and that the country should be brought into a state crisis. Nor is it a solution, however, to the relative majority proposed by the New Democracy, that is to say, the New Democracy's proposal that the President should be elected even with less than a hundred or so Members of Parliament, i.e. by a qualified majority. And that is a mistaken choice, because it is desirable that the person of the President of the Republic enjoys consensus and broad acceptance by all, particularly by the political system. We, ladies and gentlemen, propose that Article 32(4) be replaced as follows: If the increased majority needed to be extended by one year in the third vote, it is not possible to extend the term of office of the President of the Republic. Then vote shall be taken to elect the President of the Republic by a hundred and sixty votes. If the term of office of the President of the Republic has not been extended again for one year, that is to say, until the next parliamentary elections have taken place. In other words, avoid the dissolution of the House from the election of the President of the Republic. To conclude, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to refer to Rule 86 of the'subject to the responsibility of the Ministers'. This is a matter that needs attention, because in a polarisation climate events may occur unpleasantly. Of course, however, we can also avoid events that put us in a difficult position. We call 'yes' to the revision of Article 86. We say, in fact, that it is necessary, but with insurance, so as not to prosecute a weapon at the hands of the demagogues. There is, moreover, agreement between the three largest parties - SYRIZA, New Democracy and the Movement of Change - to extend the amortisation deadline of the House to prosecute two to four years, which is now more time, such as the time when the offence is cancelled. So there is the basis, we can review Article 86. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, I would say that this House is interfering with Greek constitutional history. I hope and hope that we will honour this topical process, in which we have the honour of participating. Thank you.

Finally, the nominated parliament has reached seven key points of constitutional change: Firstly, the immunity of the Member pursuant to Rule 62, who is only protected in the performance of his duties and who abolishes the amortisation deadline laid down in Article 86(3) by equating the Minister with the ordinary citizens with the limitation of criminal offences. Secondly, Rule 68 is revised for a committee of inquiry, following a proposal by ten opposition MPs and with the presence of at least a hundred twenty MPs. Thirdly, the minimum guaranteed income is constitutionally guaranteed. Fourthly, the military judges are equal to the other judges in Article 96(5) of the Constitution. Fifthly, the election of the independent authorities in accordance with Article 101a is facilitated in order to avoid phenomena such as that of the Broadcasting Council. Sixth, the vote of the Greeks abroad is facilitated. And, seventhly, the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic is separated from the parliamentary elections by amending paragraph 4 of Article 32 of the Constitution. That is what we will be dealing with, ladies and gentlemen, in the next part of my speech. The nomination of the supreme institutional rule, the President of the Republic, in other words by an absolute or relevant majority, was decided by a majority so that the democratic people's mandate for a government would not be interrupted deliberately. It is, in my opinion, a mature development of our political system, which actually removes the phenomena of the election of the President of the Republic. It is the beginning of a new period of parliamentary democracy with political stability, but also full four-year parliamentary periods, usually required for the implementation of integrated government programmes. A small retrograde to recent political history demonstrates in the best way the required revision of Article 32 of the Constitution. From 1975 until today there have been nine elections to the Presidents of the Republic. Two of them were used by PASOK in 2009 and SYRIZA in January 2015 as a tool to dismantle the parliament and provoke parliamentary elections in the middle of government periods. In other words, they have blackmailed elections in the light of their party interest and not the interests of the country. We must not forget that the 2009 and January 2015 elections may have led to the intended targeting of their inspiring, but the consequences have been paid and paid for by the Greek people and the entire country. The first time the winner of the 2009 elections Georgios Papandreou, who "removed" the Karamanlis government on the pretext of electing the President of the Republic, was blocked by his election promises. Really, who can forget "there's money"? He was obliged to take a six-month leap, which was brought by the International Monetary Fund and the two Memorandums. Five years later Alexis Tsipras, thirsty and hungry for power, as he promised everything, caused the government to fall Samaras - Venizelos again on the pretext of electing the President of the Republic in the midst of its term, and brought to the country the third and most horrific memorandum, giving the national property to creditors for a hundred years and charging all Greeks with unbearable taxes and fees. Ladies and gentlemen, this recent painful story must be learned from us. That is why we must vote unanimously in favour of the revision of Rule 32 of our Constitution to remove the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the House and the opening of early elections. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u203	Ladies and gentlemen, the revision of the country's Charter tends to turn into a story of lost opportunities, which is due either to micro-party feasibility or to the failure to form a consensual spirit between the parties. A characteristic example is the revision of Article 32 for the election of the President of the Republic. Whilst the whole political world rightly agrees that the election of the President of the Republic must be disconnected from the possibility of early recourse to the polls following the dissolution of the House, the proposal of the parliamentary majority to elect it, even by a relatively problematic majority, is also very important. I want to explain it. The person of the President of the Republic shall be condensed with the national unity. It is good to have, in a country with strong internal political division and a long history of hard party competition, a face of national reference, a person to whom our internal political divisions can resort. So let the President of the Republic express this national unity, but that can only happen through a broad democratic legitimacy of his election. It cannot be the election of the President of the Republic as a result of a party selection, a majority, in fact, of a temporary nature. He's a bad advisor to majorities, when lawmakers think they'll always be majorities. Even if we accept the assurance of the good intentions of today's majority, that he or she proposed for the post of President of the Republic will be a person of broader acceptance, the parliamentary majority should always count that after a few years another majority may not have the same good option at all. The second reason why we should not introduce a majority in this regard in the election of the President of the Republic is that we will suffer for the next few years a paradox of our Constitution: The President of an independent authority will be chosen by a four-fifths majority or - as the New Democracy is now proposing - by a three-fifths majority of the Conference of Presidents, but the President of the Republic will be chosen by a relevant majority - not even an absolute majority - by the plenary! It should not be understood that, in one case, the Conference of Presidents is chosen and, in the other, the plenary, because what matters is the rule which commits the election of the people. The rule, as far as the Conference of Presidents is concerned, is that unanimity is sought or in any case the minimum of a four-fifths majority in plenary session. Is it really possible to defend such principles? I appeal to colleagues in the New Democracy and call for a genuine withdrawal of their proposal and for a wide-ranging parliamentary majority to look together for the revision of Article 32 of the Constitution. It can be done now. My proposal is that it is sufficient to delete the fourth paragraph of Article 32 and all the other paragraphs remain - with the nomination - unchanged in terms of content, which means that the final act on the election of the President of the Republic is to form a three-fifths majority in the number of Members. The less bold and imaginable could ask: What if there is no consensus? The answer is: When the political forces know that there is no other way out, that there is no other solution, only agreement to a person who is to be elected by a three-fifths majority, will seek it. We've seen it happen to the persons who lead the independent authorities. Despite the hardship, despite the difficulties, does the Conference of Presidents, which has no escape, find a majority of four fifths or even a consensus several times? That is where we are ending, without further votes, because we must not lead to the example of the Italian Parliament and the Senate, which, with continuous votes, finally arrives to be elected by a qualified majority. Some refuse to hold referendums on a popular basis, on the grounds that the referendum usually divides the social body and leads to unwanted results. Allow me, ladies and gentlemen, to remind you of some simple truths. Firstly, all the referendums you call on, whether 2015 or the referendum in Britain, as a negative example of the division of the social body and the emergence of unwilling results, are referendums organised by the state authorities, never emerged from the popular initiative. Secondly, the expansion of participatory democracy by holding referendums on a popular initiative is not a luxury, to which we have the option of either choosing or rejecting. It's necessary. And if there are unwanted results at one time, the problem is not to those who have the initiative, that is, to the citizens, but to those who make the questions either by panic or by leaning. I would remind you - and I conclude, Madam President - that Winston Churchill, in his well-known sense of scepticism, said that 'the best argument against democracy is five minutes of discussion with the average voter'. However, it has never been understood, because of this wording, that it would abolish democracy. On the other hand, it set the conditions for discussion to improve democracy, by exercising citizens' responsibility. Finally, I will insist - and this is the proposal made by my party - that the right and happy majority - if not by unanimity - will be reviewed in Article 73 of the Constitution and that the popular legislative initiative will be provided for. I hope in one case, when we propose the popular legislative initiative, that we do not consider the people to be mature, and in the case of the initiative on the referendums to be immature. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u209	Thank you very much, Madam President. Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I will begin my speech with the citizens, noting that the concept of 'citizens' is one place in a society, but one that must be struggling for. Continuing information, lifelong learning, labour, shared participation and solidarity are only some of the things that accompany this post, the highest perhaps in a state that wants to be governed by democratic procedures. Judging now from the outset, this obligation was completely neglected as citizens, leading us to the loss of our national sovereignty, the collapse of our standard of living and the total destruction, if anyone considers, of course, as we do, that the delegation of power to some who are not elected, to the foreign surveillance of our country is synonymous with the disaster. By trying now to understand why our country remained steadfast, despite its unique natural wealth, its incomparable culture and its enormous potential, we realised that the most important obstacle to its progress was and continues to be the enmity of the Greeks towards their state. The cause of this hostility, the source essentially of all Greece's problems and malfunctions, is other than the incredible arrogance and arbitrariness of power, which literally poisons the entire social life. It is precisely this arrogance and arbitrary power that must be fought, among other things, with the help of our Constitution, if we want to look at the future with optimism, especially the future of our children, who are again condemned to exile, to emigration to other countries. In this regard, the best solution, but which can only be a continuous process with this objective, is to vote for the citizens themselves the laws they are called upon to implement at local and national level, since only so will they support them as best as possible and will be protected by the powerful. At the same time, they must elect a government and a local government, which will have an obligation to apply them, while otherwise they will be withdrawn. It is, in our view, the only way for citizens to gain confidence in the state, at least as far as Greece is concerned. This is where we recall that the New Democracy colleague said that our current political system is representative, namely that the Member does not represent the citizens, but represents the citizens. He's absolutely right, unfortunately. In simple terms, it is something quite different from the one who draws authority from the person, namely, who has to carry out exactly the commands of the citizens he represents, and another who is there instead of the person and can operate at the alleged will of the citizens in general and vaguely, as he assumes it. It is precisely for this reason that we are proposing at first instance the introduction of the institution of revocation so that the Member can operate not as he wishes, but according to the programme which he has presented and elected. Otherwise, it will be subject to revocation, on compulsory resignation. If this seems utopian to you, and if it is not in the provisions to be revised, you should know that the need for self-protection of the people from aggressive globalisation, with the absolute domination of the financial capital that now exercises and the political power, what dictates everything with the powerful weapons that it has at its disposal, with the rating companies, with the banking system, including the central banks, with speculative capital, with its infamous markets and with all its other weapons. Unfortunately, the representative political system that created globalisation has now become an instrument in its hands, against the vast majority of the world's citizens. So it has to change and it will change. In our Constitution now, in Article 1, paragraph 3, it states that all powers come from the people, there are for it and the nation and they are exercised as defined in the Constitution. In other words, while the powers come from the people, according to our constitution, they are not exercised by the people, but they simply exist for them and are exercised only by politicians, that is, as the Constitution lays down itself. In accordance with Article 918 of the Code of Civil Procedure, a judicial decision against a citizen, for example, a bank that auctions the house of a unfortunate Greek, is carried out in the name of the Greek people. How does the Greek people really get involved in all this? Does it really want the Greek people to lose their homes, the people who cannot pay the banks, who have been unemployed or paid a lot less money, because their governments overdue the country, and then went bankrupt, signing a memorandum behind the other and mortgageing it in the Penal Code? Of course not. Did the Greek people want the memos signed? Of course not. Did the Greek people want to sign the PSI? Of course not. Did the Greek people want to sell their utilities? Of course not. Did the Greek people want to get the name of our Macedonia? Of course not. Greek citizens love their homeland. They would never betray her. Therefore, the powers may indeed be derived from the citizens, but they are not exercised by the citizens, nor can they interfere with the exercise of their powers by others under the Constitution. Ultimately, the Constitution states that the powers of politicians are exercised by politicians, in complete contrast with all the other countries of Europe in which the powers not only stem from the citizens but also from the citizens, and some define the exact way in which this exercise is carried out. We will submit to the Minutes a number of corresponding provisions of other European constitutions, in order to understand the size of the Greek hypocrisy. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Vassiliou Villiardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Especially in the Portuguese Constitution, the subordination of economic power to politics is clearly mentioned, which has unfortunately been destroyed at a global level, especially in Europe, which is a micrograph of uncontrolled globalisation. Of all these examples, there is a clear discrepancy between our country and the other European countries, as far as the body of political power in Greece is concerned. Instead of correcting exactly these flaws of our Constitution, which led our country to bankruptcy, we are creating it even more over-conservative and over-concentrating. In this respect, we sincerely hope and hope that the current Prime Minister will understand what not all of us have understood, that it is a great mistake to shape the constitutions and laws in the personal measures of a Prime Minister who is today and tomorrow will not be, while our country and the citizens will be here tied up in hand and delivered in the hands of someone else who others can control. Continuing, everyone claims that after this vacant meaning Review will take 10 years to follow the next one. Really, who's imposing this? Do the lenders have until they completely reap the country? Does anyone really think that this Constitution will last for the next ten years, which are expected to be very serious events worldwide, in a bankrupt country, how much more so when Greece's continued settlement is under way with illegal immigrants who are gradually altering the structure of our society? With regard to certain basic provisions of the Constitution which are the subject of today's debate, I would like to mention the following. The rules of international law and international organisations are set out in Article 28(2). In this respect, we believe that every international treaty that relates to our national sovereignty or alters our national characteristics must be ratified by a referendum. We therefore propose a new wording of this paragraph as follows: "Every international treaty or international agreement which, firstly, recognises the institutions of international organizations provided for by the Constitution, secondly, grants or restricts the exercise of national sovereignty, thirdly, alters the historical, cultural and religious path of Hellenism, is required to be ratified by a referendum in which at least 50% of the electorate will participate. Otherwise, the Treaty or the Agreement shall not apply.’ If there were this provision in our Constitution, there would be no memoranda and there would have been no vote on the Prespecies Agreement, while every foreign factor would never be aware of pressing the Greek governments. Simplifiedly, the more centralised the political system - and with the Constitution today will become even more centralised - the more easily the foreign factor breaks down a country. In Article 44 on referendums, with the aim of introducing institutions of direct democracy and citizens' participation in decision-making, we propose that the paragraph should be changed as follows: "The Resolution is compulsory by the President of the Republic, when the House so requests by an absolute majority of the total number of Members of Parliament, or if they request signatures of a hundred thousand citizens". I will testify in the Minutes what applies to other states, so that it can be understood that we have not even discovered the wheel here. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Vassiliou Villiardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] In Article 73, in the right to propose laws, in order to introduce directly democratic institutions and citizens' participation in decision-making, we propose to change the entire article as follows: "The right to propose laws belongs to the Parliament, the government and the citizens. A proposal in the House to vote or to amend a law by citizens is legal, since it includes an accurate draft law after its explanatory statement and is accompanied by signatures of thirty thousand citizens. Introduction to debate and vote in Parliament shall take place in accordance with the procedure laid down for the proposals for legislation of the Government and of the Parliament." I will also submit to the Minutes what applies to other states, so that it can be understood that neither we have discovered the wheel here. It applies in too many other countries. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Vassiliou Villiardos, presents for the minutes the above document, which is in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] I shall close with Rule 32(4), election of the President of the Republic. The proposal of the Greek Solution is stable, not today, and it is this: the President of the Republic should be elected directly by the citizens, following proposals from either ten MPs or thirty-three thousand citizens and, indeed, the President of the Republic should certainly return the powers he had during the 1975 Constitution and even more extensive. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u213	Thank you, Madam President. We have again, ladies and gentlemen, on the occasion of the objection of unconstitutionalism tabled by the SYRIZA Parliamentary Group on the question of the limits of competence of the two Bulgarians, the proposer and the decisive one, but an issue which has been discussed sufficiently well during the previous parliamentary term and is wisely formulated by the Constitution that wants the two Bulgarians to be equivalent: one, the proposing one, to determine the agenda for the revision and the revision, to decide on the content of these provisions. And the fact that the electorate is being interfered with by elections gives the power to the Review House to do exactly that, that is, to define the content of the provisions that are being revised. There is therefore no question of interpreting this issue, for which top constitutionalists have in the past answered such as Dimitrios Tsatsos and Aristovoulos Menesis. Ladies and gentlemen, the Constitution of our country is the fundamental, supreme law of the state, which affects more than anyone else's lives. Anyone can understand why our Constitution must be modern and flexible, meet the requirements of Greek society and integrate the real social demands. One of these requests, which is also one of the top and most important institutional changes that is being attempted by the current Review House, is the request to disconnect the emergence of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament, which the vast majority of Greek citizens demand. The reason why little - we all know that. The provision of Article 32(4), which provides for the early dismantling of the Parliament in the event of the President of the Republic being unable to elect, was the political alibi, the pretext for our country to move to early elections, with, of course, lasting at the end of 2014, where SYRIZA, in a climate of total political depreciation and extreme division, severely ceased the upward course of the economy and our country, with all known consequences. Ladies and gentlemen, our democracy can no longer be a commitment to the political strategy in question. Governments should be able to complete their mandate, implement their work in four years' time and on this basis they should also be judged by the Greek people. The wide-ranging parliamentary convergence which has been noted in both the Pro-Government and the Revision Committee on this matter demonstrates the need for a revision of Article 32(4) for a new way of highlighting the President of the Republic, which will give a definitive end to the paralysing phenomena of the past. The proposal of the New Democracy is clear, concrete and restored to institutional balance. The election of the Head of State shall be disconnected from the controversy and the Parliament shall be given the opportunity to deal with the choice of the person of the President of the Republic with the institutional responsibility for it. So, according to our proposal, we are eliminating every margin for wider consensus, effectively enabling all the political forces to reach a face of mutual acceptance in three successive votes. However, when this is not possible, we shall proceed with an election procedure with an absolute or relative majority, which is also the rule at international level. On the contrary, the solution proposed by the Qualified Opposition is lacking not only reasonable but also a legal basis. The SYRIZA proposes successive votes in a long-lived process, which lasts for a six-month period, and if they are fruitless, direct election by the President of the Republic by the people. First of all, this solution lacks a legal basis. The framework within which the new way of giving the President is to be defined shall be laid down by Article 30(1) of the Constitution, which states that the President of the Republic shall be elected by the Parliament and not by the people or by any other body. However, that provision is not part of the revised provisions and remains as it stands. Any solution proposed should therefore be in line with the provision of Article 30(1). Furthermore, a possible direct election of the President of the Republic by the people would create serious issues as well as in the form of our own constitution, which is based on Article 1 of the Constitution, the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic. The above provision of Article 1 belongs to the provisions of the Constitution which are not revised. SYRIZA's proposal attempts to establish a peculiar'semi-chronic' system, with two parallel legalities, the elected Parliament and the elected President of the Republic, but a logic that is inconsistent with our culture. As for the long-term process of the six-month period and the successive votes, one can easily see the enormous political upheaval and destabilisation that can also cause the dangers of our country's political stability and security. To close, ladies and gentlemen, it may be with the sole responsibility of SYRIZA and his ideas that we have lost a great opportunity to have a radical revision of the Constitution, such as the revision of Article 16 or Article 24. Nevertheless, the new Article 32(4) and the decoupling of the election of the President of the Republic from the early dissolution of Parliament is a victory and an important conquest for our democracy. The new way of showing the President shall ensure that he is over-partyd and dismissed from any political confrontation. It respects the President's institutional role, but also ensures the smooth functioning and continuation of the other institutions of the country. It eliminates all margins of consensus from all political forces, but it also offers a realistic solution if this is not achieved, because, as the President of our party and Prime Minister of Greece Kyriakos Mitsotakis said, when consensus is not reached and increased majority, the President of the Republic cannot turn into a division. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u218	Thank you, Mr. Comrade. I will now give the floor to the Parliamentary Representative of the Change Movement, Mr Kamine, for 15 minutes and immediately thereafter to Mrs Iatridis.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u219	Thank you, Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen, we are concerned today with issues which, because they are at the core of the parliamentary culture, have repeatedly caused marginal political conflicts. They have marked the political life of the country, already in the middle of the 19th century. In particular, the issue of the position and responsibilities of the Head of State has shaken political life for decades, caused a national division in 1915 and half a century later, a second that led to the imposition of the April dictatorship. The relocation had to come so that, with the 1974 referendum, the issue of the form of the constitution and the choice of the Unsuccessful Democracy could be resolved. The 1986 Review then abolished the so-called superpowers of the President of the Republic, so that it finally ended a great loose end, which had shaken the political life of the country for six decades. Since then it has become undeniable that Greece's civilisation is a parliamentarian of a monetaristic nature, that is to say, the government depends on the confidence of the House and not on the President of the Republic, as he was letting our Constitution to be transparent before the 1986 Review. Finally, the constitutional theory could now turn its attention to other issues, such as the introduction of institutional counterweights to the enhanced power of the government and Prime Minister. So, with all honesty, I was very surprised to hear the SYRIZA's proposal that the President of the Republic should be elected immediately by the people if the votes in this House were to be fruitless. Firstly, because this is contrary to Article 30(1) of the Constitution which stipulates that the President of the Republic is elected by the House and obviously only means by the House. Here, then, it is not possible to circumvent the inconsistency of Officer Response. Because while he maintains that the Review House is strictly bound by the directions of the proposal, he comes and proposes to us today that we move on to indirect and unarmed, that is, to smuggling, a revision of Article 30 of the Constitution, which is not even included in the provisions under review. The proposal by SYRIZA, however, also contradicts Article 110(1) of the Constitution, which stipulates that the provisions setting the basis and form of the culture as the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic shall not be reviewed. If, ladies and gentlemen, this proposal were to prevail, we would actually introduce a semi-presidential system, since we would eventually end up with the government's dependence and the confidence of a powerful one, since it would be elected by the people, the President of the Republic. We'd actually be back for a century. Inevitably we would have a President of the Republic directly elected by the people and therefore, equipped with popular legitimacy, corresponding to the House. And there is no need to be particularly imaginative to assume that sooner or later we would be in conflict, between the parliamentary majority and the people elected by the President of the Republic. We would, in other words, reach a state crisis. With SYRIZA's proposal, we would actually fly into the disuse basket, the 1986 Review, which no one is now questioning. On the other hand, however, we have the proposal of the New Democracy which leads to the degree of opposite effect. The current majority opens up the possibility of the President of the Republic at some point by a simple majority of the Members present. In other words, we will have a Head of State with reduced authority. The word 'principal', ladies and gentlemen, is of great importance here, because it refers to the distinction between autoritas and potestas. Potestas is power, power, power of enforcement. Most of the state bodies of the legislative and executive authorities exercise potestas, starting with the parliament which lays down the general rules of law that impose on us or prohibit us or allow us something to the last public official, who, as a member of a disciplinary board, imposes the penalty of dismissal on his colleague. The legislative and executive bodies shall lay down and implement corresponding rules of law with the power of enforcement. The legalisation of this coercion in our legal order draws it from the democratic principle, especially since the majority, which requires confrontation and conflict. And precisely because, in our culture, conflict exists as a derivative of ideological and political confrontation, the Constitution as a counterbalance also provides for some other instruments that equip them with autoritas, that is with authority or authority. It's the automaticity in English. These instruments are surrounded by guarantees of political objectivity and impartiality in order to keep them, as far as possible, out of political controversy. It is such institutions that make up the judiciary and the independent authorities. I will not, however, deal with this today, but with the third body which has to enjoy prestige: with the President of the Republic. Since the party conflict may in marginal situations of polarisation and division threaten the foundations that support and continue the state, it is necessary for a President of the Republic who has not as much powers as the prestige, that is, according to the Constitution, to give it an increased parliamentary majority, which goes beyond the limits of the government. Because the task of the President of the Republic is to break above the political conflict as a regulator of the constitution, as the Constitution explicitly provides. It is he, that is, who is called upon to restore the normal pace of the civil war, when that rate is disturbed because of the seriousness of the political conflict. In fact, in order to strengthen the regulatory role of the President of the Republic, we are proposing today that he, without the Prime Minister's signature, should be able to address proclamations and convene the Council of Political Heads. These are primarily regulatory responsibilities. In other words, the proposal of the New Democracy is in complete contrast with the regulator's very mission, as it is now apparent from the Constitution. In addition, he considers SYRIZA's proposal to necessarily possess the status of a Member of Parliament, a proposal that coincides with the unexplained empathy that SYRIZA seems to feed against Mr. Luke Papademos, a modest man, an internationally distinguished technocrat, who literally saved the country. Nor does the constructive motion for distrust today have any interest. However, we must recognise that both of these proposals of the SYRIZA are closely interlinked with the simple analogue. As experience has shown in Greece and internationally, the system of mere proportionalness often leads - not always, but often - to compromise governments, which embodies this compromise in the face of a party-coloured technocrat, but that, as we have seen, does not want SYRIZA. The constructive motion of censure is also closely intertwined with the mere proportional system. It aims to ensure the government's stability threatened by the fragmentation of the parliament caused by simply proportional. In other words, it obliges the political forces which agree to overthrow a government, to have the alternative ready, and that is why the proposal is also being called constructive. I have tabled my opinion on the proposal for referendums. The experience of the 2015 referendum is a black page in the post-cultural history of the place. This referendum suffers as to how it was conducted, as to the question he raised and ultimately as to the fate that the government then gave to the people's verdict. I believe that the only reason why the President of the Republic has come to this project was not to upgrade to a state crisis the extreme political conflict that he had broken out with the announcement of the referendum by the then Prime Minister. I would like to dwell on Article 73 and on the proposal made by the Change Movement for the so-called legislative initiative. We propose, with five hundred thousand signatures, that proposals for legislation be put before the House and that the President should be forced to table a debate in plenary. As our general rapporteur, Andreas Loverdes, has said, with our proposal, a new political right is created, which is a reasoned deepening of democracy and is fully consistent with the parliamentary character of the culture. On the basis of the table submitted by the chairman of the Committee, Mrs Stylianides, the popular legislative initiative is set out in twenty one of the forty-seven of the Council of Europe, ten of which are countries of the European Union. To conclude, ladies and gentlemen, let me stress that the debate on the election of the President of the Republic is extremely enlightening because it brings to light the great problem of our political life, the inability to agree at last that some fundamental institutions and rules of political life must remain unavoidable. That will never be ensured by reviewing the Constitution, with all the guarantees that we can put in place if we surround the new provisions that we are voting on. We have the illusion and we are transmitting it to the people, as far as we are able to keep to it, that by reforming the fundamental law of the state, we can automatically improve the quality of political controversy. That's a big misunderstanding. Great constitutional reforms are those that either pave the way for the joint legislator to coordinate his stepping up with social development, or open the game to new political forces that the establishment kept on the margin. Those changes which simply come in order to change constitutional rules not because they are suffering themselves, but because they have been erroneously implemented by political parties, are of no use, because the problem here is not constitutional but political. It's a matter of political morals. After all, it is a matter of restraint of the political forces. At some point in this country we should realise that democracy includes the political conflict which ensures that power and the renewal of political life are changed, but this conflict also has its limits. Here comes the moral responsibility for the actions of the political forces, expressed by the restraint to which I referred earlier. But that will not teach us any constitutional theory and no constitution. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u227	Ladies and gentlemen, in fact the debate on the Revision of the Constitution revolves around the issue of the election of the President of the Republic. [[At this point the IV Vice-President of Parliament, Mr DIMITRIOS VITSAS, takes over the presidential office.]] There are other changes which are proposed and which have secured the majority required to go ahead. However, this review is another missed opportunity to change some very important things that could change. We, of course, have proposed them in the Proposed House, but they have not been accepted. One and very important, in my view, is the missing opportunity to change Article 16 on the functioning of non-governmental university institutions. It was a necessary arrangement, at last in 2019, which, unfortunately, again because of the ideas of the Left Party parties, did not go ahead. We accept a reality, we accept many of the Greeks leaving abroad, paying there money. We do not accept coming to study in private Greek universities children from other states and strengthening the Greek economy, precisely because we are stuck in an idealism of decades past. It is therefore a lost opportunity in this regard. Ladies and gentlemen, our Constitution of 1974-75 has a dogmatic, legal and political unity regarding the establishment and functioning of the state. Paragraph 1 of Article 1 clearly stipulates that Greece is a Presiding Parliamentary Republic. The fundamental principle of our culture is the parliamentary authority, as well as the principle of presiding. But what does a parliamentary authority mean? That the centre of gravity of political life is the House. It's the House that's giving government. The government must enjoy Parliament's confidence on the basis of the principle of declared. Parliament also elects the President of the Republic under Rule 30. Is it random? Oh, no. It is precisely on the basis of the parliamentary authority that Parliament is the centre of our political democratic life. The 1975 Constitution, as it was first based, clearly had elements of the Presidential Republic, despite the fact that a direct election of the President of the Republic was foreseen by the parliament. I would remind you that the President could then appoint and dismiss the government on the basis of the old Rules 37 and 38, to dismantle the House when he found himself obvious discomfort between society and Parliament. She had the right to dismantle the House if she had voted against two governments. He had the right to refer a bill that was passed by parliament. He had the right to make a proclamation without taking into account anyone's opinion and still putting the country in siege. All these elements of the President-in-Office which, in my personal view, did not correspond to the principle of the President-in-Office, were removed from our Constitution by the 1985-1986 constitutional revision. The new Constitution has limited the powers of the President, who has remained a regulator of the constitution under Article 30, to a fully standardised form of procedure. Everything, that is, from how the government is sworn by the President, from how it takes the mandate, up to the dissolution of the House, is now completely standardized. Dimitrios Tsatsos in one of his writings, based precisely on the democratic principle and the principle of presiding, to which I referred, states: "This principle excludes those forms of reviewing the institution of the supreme ruler, which creates the institution of the function...", i.e. the President, "...substantial institutional intervention in the state's become". However, Article 110(1), which refers to the Revision of the Constitution and to what provisions can be considered, excludes those provisions which define the basis and form of the constitution as a Presiding Parliamentary Republic. In this sense, therefore, SYRIZA's proposal, which tells us to go to the people in case there is a deadlock in the election, is to the limits of today's constitutional order, the President-in-Office, because it is clear that, with the election by the people of a President of the Republic, this automatically gives the body of power and power, which, based on the overall spirit and the letter of the Constitution, does not have. In addition, SYRIZA proceeded with a proposal, without taking into account Article 30. It accuses us today of unconstitutionalism - and that was one of the issues we discussed, on the basis of our own proposal - and it does not tell us how it would deal with this dimension that would exist within the Constitution, where Article 30 would remain and would say that the President is elected by Parliament and in Article 32 we would do a procedure in which we would end up with the people, namely that the House would not elect it, but the people. It is an obvious disharmony between two provisions of the Constitution, which should somehow be resolved. In practice, how would that be? The only proposal, ladies and gentlemen, after all these thoughts, which are in line with today's general spirit, but also the letter of the Constitution, is the proposal of the New Democracy. Because, first of all, it fits with what Article 30 says, that the President is elected by the people. Secondly, it does not go beyond the limits laid down in Article 110(1) on revised provisions, i.e. it certainly does not give the President, the President and the city, the elements of presidential democracy, which the SYRIZA's proposal does. He also said Mr Kegeroglu, in a sharp manner earlier, that we are being accused by saying 'But will you elect a president with a relative majority?'. And today, after the deadlock and after the election, Rule 32(4), which we are reviewing, provides for a majority. It has simply intervened in an entire election, which has the following dangerous element: the President's election enters the party, political controversy and goes to the people, the parties certainly take a stand in favour of the person, so that when we reach the final election, this person, instead of expressing the unity of the nation, on the basis of the constitution, can express a division, a divisional reality. For all these reasons, ladies and gentlemen, I think that the New Democracy proposal, which provides for a large majority, is a challenge for the political forces. Since, in fact, they will know that they will not go to elections, it is really a challenge to find the right person, which will guarantee a large majority - not a hundred and eighty, but a hundred seats that are needed in the first vote - so that the person that has those elements, who unite the Greek people, who will not divide him and who will always be the real regulator of our society, can be elected. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u229	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the election of the President of the Republic is one of the top procedures of Greek Parliament. The way of raising and indirect election of the Head of State play a very essential role. Today we are discussing one of the top changes of this Constitutional Review, which concerns the election of President of the Republic, Rule 32(4) and 5 and its disconnection from the dissolution of the House. The three initial votes remain the increased majority, with a view to the widest possible consensus, as has been the case so far. If they do not, the fourth vote provides for an absolute majority in the number of Members, i.e. a hundred and fifty. Although this proves to be fruitless, it remains the insurance policy of displaying and by a relative majority, which is essentially the last solution, as already provided for in the current Constitution. There will just be no more election mediation. In any case, the major is resolved. The disconnection of the presidential election procedure from the dissolution of the House and the premature recourse to the polls. This is a major political change. In the wake of the opposition, the weapon of early elections will be given the opportunity for the government-led majority, with a people's mandate, to complete its mandate and not be threatened by political manoeuvres. We all know what happened in 2014, when SYRIZA abused the institutions for its own benefit, in order to cast the Samara government. The circumventing and working of the election process lies in the behaviour of the Officerial Representation to refuse to vote for a person, even if he considers it capable of taking office, only to create a deadlock that will lead to the compulsory dissolution of the House. This is what SYRIZA did when he refused to vote in favour of the nominated candidate, Stavros Dimas, without proposing an alternative, simply aiming at an early election. His attitude was dictated by party purposes, in view of what served his interests at that particular time. He led the country to forced elections, which brought about a change in the correlation between parliamentary forces. This political and constitutionally credible behaviour has today led almost all the political forces, including those who have taken the worst advantage of Article 32(4), to converging for the revision of this article. The common location of the proposals is the removal of the President's election from the compulsory dismantling of the House. There are, of course, different approaches to the way the deadlock is removed. The government's majority, while respecting the institutions, is proposing the most realistic solution. SYRIZA's proposal for a direct election may sound philosophical. But it is an incomplete and incomplete solution. It is problematic, because it turns a parliamentary process of indirect election into a direct one, creating issues relating to the functioning and form of the culture. While we are looking for an increased majority and consensus, with direct election by the people, we are leading to the electoral conflict. The institutional perception of the Head of State is changed and could potentially lead to political instability and demilitarisation of the government in a major state crisis. The role of our Constitution for the President is not to govern, but to guarantee political stability. We are proposing the only possible solution to enable it to be achieved by avoiding possible adventures. The supra-party and united role of the supra-party ruler must be ensured and not be risky to end up dangerous and biasing. In conclusion, by making a comprehensive assessment, I would like to point out that this Review could change much more. While the New Democracy proposal was to have a debate on everything, with a look at the future, unfortunately, the previous parliament has limited the framework for the review process. For example, it did not dare to touch the crucial article 16, the question of the establishment of private universities, a self-evident necessity for the Greek society that has been a central proposal of the New Democracy on a matter that keeps Greece very back in relation to the rest of Europe for many years. Another opportunity has been lost for the Constitution to keep pace with the country's modern educational needs. Even in this narrow context, therefore, the government majority with coolness and a high sense of responsibility suggests provisions for a Constitution that is functional, its character and its procedures will not be the barbaric of party planning and will not be altered in due course. The traumatic experiences of manipulation of the institution in the past demonstrate in the most obvious way that our political system has proved to be inferior to the intentions of the legislator. The decoupling of the president's election from the dissolution of the House is a great institutional change of fundamental importance for our democratic city. No more party conflicts for a person we want to be a symbol of unity and national understanding. It is good that today in this Review almost all parties are converging even if they prefer different solutions. I really hope that in future the President of the Republic will become a climate of consensus with wider parliamentary majorities. This requires the role and the form of our culture, the President-in-Office of the Parliamentary Republic. Thank you.

We are in favour of the committees that will aim to investigate and ultimately render political and only responsibilities from Parliament in the footsteps of the American impeachment followed by Congress. In no case, however, would we agree to dismantle the powers created by the pre-review committees. This task lies only with the judicial officers, who can interrogate, examine, condemn and punish whenever necessary. It is incomprehensible and none of us would have consented to the institutionalisation - and indeed the constitutionalisation of Parliament. We are here as representatives of the Greek people to listen to their needs and problems, to seek and try to legislate on them and not to be here, acting as interrogators and judges. We are therefore opposed to any work, using the committees of inquiry and pre-review, as a means of party controversy and instrument for fulfilling micropolitical objectives. This body would have full independence from the executive, of course it would be made up of public prosecutors to ensure that any action requires public intervention or a warrant, such as the opening of a bank account by the qualified police personnel who would have the necessary knowledge of new technologies for data collection and analysis, the monitoring of cash and all money paths. Ladies and gentlemen, as a PART 25, as I said before, we consider the involvement of citizens to be extremely important either through parliamentary bodies or through binding referendums. Such referendums we propose that the ruling of the sovereign people should be added to Article 28 on the ratification of important national interest agreements and on the allocation of increased competences to international organisations, as in 44, with our proposal on the basis of the belief that it is necessary for the sovereign people to decide, by expressing their will in referendums which are binding and which will take place on matters of high interest in the country and following a decision by the absolute majority of the whole number of Members. In the eighth section, the main article concerns the election of the President of the Republic. And here we believe that under no circumstances should the President of the Republic function in a pretext and actually become the pawn of a micro-political balance game, as we have seen happening several times in our recent history, and his unsuccessful election to lead to the dissolution of this House and subsequent election. We believe that political stability must be safeguarded in every way and that the state institution of the President of the Republic operates as a pillar of strengthening participatory democracy. Therefore, our proposal is, if we do not achieve an increased majority in the third ballot, then a special electorate should be set up in the PPC model, which will in this case be made up of the three hundred active MPs and twice as large as six hundred, confiscated citizens, who will vote and elect the President of the Republic. This underlines the institutional unified role and removes any possibility of provoking political instability and vague governmentality that the dissolution of this House and the subsequent elections could cause. At the same time, with our proposal, the popular will can be expressed without having a direct election, which would ultimately have the opposite effect, since the President of the Republic would end up, although in fact it does not have enough powers, taking more votes than the Prime Minister of the country. The text to which we will end up should help and make it easier for everyone to understand that the Constitution is not a dry, incomprehensible legal text, but an intelligible set of provisions that everyone can understand without having to have legal knowledge. Ladies and gentlemen, the spirit that prevailed in the debate and the resolution of the 1975 Constitution, as in most of the following review efforts, was basically consensual, as is appropriate in such an important process. That is why, as Mera 25, we have come to the committee with proposals rather than sterile criticism and opposition. The Constitution of our country cannot and must in no way become a field of micro-political discrimination and disagreements. At the same time, as I said, the final revised Constitution must have been modernised to disarm the needs of our time, the lessons that we have gathered from all these hard years since the 2008 Review to date, years that brought our country into the lap of the memoranda and the policies of a sterile austerity that finally turned our country into a devastated debt colony that now has to stand on its feet and strip off all the bonds that keep it back. Undoubtedly, the 1975 Constitution was quite advanced for its time, but since then, several decades have passed and the data have changed. Therefore, all these structural and institutional changes should, we believe, be reflected in the final revised text of the Constitution. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-20-S1-commons.u272	Ladies and gentlemen, it is a great honour for me as a new member of the Greek Parliament to begin my parliamentary term with the participation in the exercise of the review procedure, namely the competence of the Revision House to formulate the new structure of the charter of our constitution. At the same time, it is now a joint observation that the constitutional review should have as its main concern the improvement of the effectiveness of the culture and the deepening of our democracy. The recommendation to change the provision of Article 32(4), which provides for the dismantling of the Parliament in the event of failure to elect the President of the Republic, shall serve as a pretext. Under the current provision, the constitutional legislature sought to reach a broader parliamentary consensus between the political parties on the face of the President of the Republic, who, according to the Constitution, is also the regulator of the constitution. Unfortunately, the above provision was exploited and circumvented by specific political forces, to achieve short-term party profits, but with incalculable long-term costs for the country and the Greek society. Both in 2009 and 2014, the then-time opposition officer parties chose to "work" the presidential election, defying the constitutional check to achieve wider consensus. By acting manifestly uninstitutionally, they refused their effective participation in the process, thus leading the country to early elections. The intervention in Article 32 of the Constitution and the disconnection of the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament is curing in this respect a fundamental institutional weakness which exists today in the country's constitutional charter. It is also particularly good that there has now been a wider consensus in the revision of the article both by political parties and by Greek society. However, at the present stage of the process of mainstreaming the new framework for the election of the President of the Republic in a way that will prevent irresponsible political forces from succeeding and "working" democratic institutions for their own benefit in future. Unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen, once again the Qualified Opposition refuses to be taught by the mistakes of the recent past. Its proposal for the election of the President of the Republic conceals micro-party purposes and is also characterised by a strong constitutionalism. More specifically, the introduction of six successive votes in the House and recourse to direct election of the President of the Republic by the people will result in the political system being caught in an environment of endless party conflicts and polarization, with negative consequences for ensuring the political stability of the country. At the same time, SYRIZA's proposal makes the institution of the President of the Republic entirely dependent on the party support, which clearly violates the Constitution, which requires for the supreme state ruler his role as a symbol of unity of the Greek people. On the contrary, our proposal, the New Democracy's proposal for a new procedure for the election of the President of the Republic, is clearly and absolute institutionalism. Four votes in Parliament, in which the first two hundred votes are required, in the third hundred and eighty and in the fourth hundred and fifty-one, ensure this necessary political time, so that the parliamentary parties can reach the consensus required by the spirit of the Constitution. At the same time, with the introduction of a simple majority in the fourth vote, the stability of the electoral cycle, which was the main reason why Article 32 is revised, is ensured. To conclude, the current review process, due to the ideas and the atomity of the parliamentary majority in the proposed parliament, has not included critical reform proposals for changing articles of the Constitution in a direction of institutional relaunching our culture and ensuring a long-term development logic. Nevertheless, the revision of Article 32 is an exception to that reality, which is why it is a first-class opportunity to have a positive change of long-term breath for the functioning of our political system. [[At this point, the F Vice-President of Parliament, Mr GEORGIOS LAMPROYLIS, takes over the office of President.]] A wider consensus on this issue between the parliamentary parties can be the starting-up of a new, more hopeful and optimistic era for our country and our democratic civilisation. Thank you very much.
